- **Migrations:** `f3a7c2d9e1b4` (harbours and journeys), `d4e8f1a2b6c9` (the 100m is Invention), `e7b1c4d8a2f6` (a journey is not a voyage), `a9c3e5b7d1f2` (a stage's cards). The full suite is 297 tests.

## A plan's version: 1.2.3 — the journey, the content, the parameters (8 November, 21:50, Justin: "we need a numbering version for runs. with 1.2.3 where major version is the the journey of the ship, so order of the pretraining and postraining, the minor version is the content and the last number can be something else")

- **The scheme** (`thorp/versioning.py`, `s1.run_versions`, migration `d2e4c1a8b3f9`):
  - **Major — the journey**: the stages a ship lives through, in order. Every voyage today is `["pretrain"]`, so v1; a tutor, a post-training stage or a fine-tune makes a new journey and a new major. The order *within* pretraining (metre before content, or content before metre) is a parameter, not a journey — it changes the patch.
  - **Minor — the content**: the corpus list *and its bytes*, the tokeniser (kind, vocabulary, what it was trained on) and the cleaning. A new corpus version, or a list that adds a tutor's passages, is a new minor.
  - **Patch — everything else that changes the plan**: shape, context, optimiser, passes, the scaffold's cards and schedule, a teacher. **A seed is not a version**: two seeds are one plan sailed twice.
- **Assigned on first sight, never reused.** `thorp version SPEC [--assign]` shows a plan's fingerprints and its version; a new journey takes the next major (minor and patch 0), a new content the next minor within its major, anything else the next patch. A run does this itself at launch and logs it (`train._run`), so the numbering never depends on a person remembering.
- **The register as it stands:** `1.0.0` the SOP's 300k launch, `1.0.1` its 1m, `1.0.2` its 9m, `1.0.3` its 21m, `1.0.4` its 50m (one content, one journey, five plans); `1.0.5` the 300k's four-card arm; `1.1.0` the 9m on Ogden **v0.2** (a new content, a new minor — and patch 0, because it is the first plan on it); `1.2.0` the 1m on **v0.1 + the maths tutor** (another content).
- **What it closes:** a *name* is not unique in the ledger — there are three runs called `fu-ogden-v0-1@1m-pre-s1`, two recalled stubs from the morning and the finished voyage at 21:38 — and a reader who takes the first by name compares a finished voyage with a three-minute one. A version identifies the **plan**; the run id identifies the execution; a model's identity is now `(version, seed)`.
- **Ops on the way:** the other hand's migration made a second Alembic head, so `upgrade head` failed everywhere (including the test fixture). Merged with `213865d7b61d`, one head again. Tried by `tests/test_versioning.py` (7 tests: the journey, a seed is not a version, a parameter, a corpus, a scaffold, idempotence, and the patch/minor counting).

- **A rescore was a "new voyage" to the gate (found at 02:42, fixed 03:20).** Three `thorp eval` commands on finished runs were refused by the fitout policy: `train.rescore` changes the spec's eval sets (and evaluators) before calling `prepare`, and `run_id(spec)` hashes the spec, so the changed spec no longer matched the run in the ledger — `prepare`'s "is this run known?" test said no, the gate ran, and the Babel runs named a fitout that is not registered. Nothing was wrong with the readings. `train.prepare` now takes `known: bool | None`, and `rescore` passes `known=True`: a rescore is an existing run by definition, and every real voyage is still gated exactly as before. Verified by re-running one of the three (`--evaluators shapes`): it read `shapes/keyed_top1=0.2083` at step 8216. The other two are re-queued.

## Four decisions from the morning: chaining is the fleet's goal, content-first is v0.0.2, the box is fixed, and the 300k's heads wait on the hypothesis (9 November, 06:25, Justin: "chaining lift is the goal across all ships in the fleet, so ultimately to earn launching the 100m, we only earn launching the 100m when we get chaining as high as possible across the fleet. Content first should be 0.2. For 300k more heads it depends on the current hypothesis. fix the boxs provisioning")

- **Chaining lift is the fleet's goal, and the 100m is earned by it.** Every size keeps its own goal (Pan the bigram table, Augustus the maths book), but the measure across the fleet is chaining: a size is doing its job when its chaining lift is as high as that size can reach, and **the 100m sails only when chaining is as high as possible across the fleet** — not on a schedule or a budget. The crown was already decided on chaining lift (0.03 noise, two sailings), so the doctrine and the register now agree. SOP §6 and §8's first question are answered.
- **Content-first is SOP v0.0.2**, seeded and current. Three phases as data: `[{content 0.40}, {scaffold 0.40, hold 0.25, fade 1.0}, {content 0.20}]` — the ship reads first, the cars are pulled in at 40% of the voyage, the pull is zero by 80%, and the last fifth is by heart. Same content, same parameters, same five cards; **the order is the whole of the version** (`ships/plans/sop-v0.0.2.md`, `ships/plans/sop-v0.0.2.json`). Its first voyage is queued: `fu-ogden-v0-1~sop002@300k-pre-s1` (version 1.0.10), before the census block, with the prediction pre-registered: previous_word at least v0.0.1's 0.761, delimiter above the control's 3.42, the Stage 1s no worse.
- **The box's provisioning is fixed at the root.** The hired box's `uv sync --group torch` was fetching `en_core_web_sm` — a model wheel hosted on GitHub — because it sat in the base dependencies, though only the tagger (building a shapes lexicon) ever loads it and a box never builds one. It is now the `tagger` group: `uv tree --group torch` no longer contains it (checked), the Mac installs it explicitly (`--group tagger`), and a 504 from GitHub can no longer hold a box's set-up. That was the 50m's failure last night: hired, idle 54 minutes, released.
- **The 300k's heads wait on the hypothesis, and the hypothesis is written down.** The night's evidence points at capacity — four heads pinned on four heads halved the delimiter (3.42 → 1.68) while the cards that were missing barely moved — so `300k-h4` is the test *of that hypothesis*. But the fleet's goal is chaining, and more width does not buy the bridge: the card resolves in the middle layers, which is depth, not heads. So h4 is held as a **card-capacity test**, not a chaining bet, and it does not jump the queue until the census says whether the cards move chaining at all.

## The reasoning thesis: why chaining doesn't scale, and the gate it earns (9 November, 06:45, Justin: "so then our experiments should be why isn't chaining scaling up our ladder" … "this the priority for the next 50m. the queue must be reprioritised to get a thesis on how to scale reasoning up the fleet. So once we get a hypothesis, we then test it on the next SOP version across the fleet and if we can prove chaining scales from 300k to 1m to 9m to 21m, then we've earnt another 50m")

- **The problem, in numbers.** The fleet's best chaining lift is **0.085** — a 300k census arm, one sailing — against a performed mark of 0.1. By size it is 0.085 / 0.034 / 0.048 / 0.008 / 0.017: flat, noise-level, and *not* a ladder. The field: Qwen3 4B 0.538, Phi-4-mini 0.348, Llama 3.2 3B 0.285, Gemma 3 4B 0.254, SmolLM3 0.253, Gemma 2 2B 0.220, phi-2 0.202, Qwen3 1.7B 0.144, Pythia 410M 0.077, Qwen3 0.6B 0.074. **We sit at Qwen3-0.6B's level with a 300k** — a real result at a thousandth of the parameters, and nowhere near the 2–4B band the claim needs (0.20–0.50 at 300–400M).
- **The measure is not the first suspect.** The four chaining tests order the same way for a 4B, a 0.6B and us — pigeonhole > between > mill > nature — so our ships are doing a weaker version of the same thing, not something alien. And our mean is one test: pigeonhole 0.251 does all the work, while **nature reads −0.038**, the one test Qwen3 0.6B still passes (0.066).
- **A cheap hypothesis was tested and ruled out.** The endings' tokenisation does not explain nature: under our 2048 BPE the rival ending is *finer* than the idea at every test (nature +0.38 tokens, between +0.46, pigeonhole +0.17, mill 0.00), which would favour the idea, not the rival. The negative nature lift is not an artefact of the endings.
- **The programme, in order:** (1) score **every checkpoint** of the 9m, the 21m and the 50m on the chaining tests — free, no training, the three reads at the head of the queue — to see whether chaining appears mid-voyage and decays (as the bridge did) or never appears at all; (2) from that, one hypothesis about why it doesn't scale; (3) test it on the **next SOP version across the fleet** (300k, 1m, 9m, 21m); (4) **only if chaining is shown to scale from 300k to 1m to 9m to 21m does the 50m get earned**, to see whether the scaling holds there. That is the gate on the next big hire, on top of the SOP §6 rule that the 100m waits for fleet chaining.
- **The queue is reprioritised:** the three chaining reads now head it, the maths shares and the v0.0.2 arm follow, and the **69 census arms are moved to the tail** — they answer whether shelves move the cards, not whether chaining scales. Still queued, not dropped.

## The depth test: the copy circuit at 67% depth in the smallest class (9 November, 07:28, Justin: "this is the biggest priority test. can we add this depth to the 300k")

- **What prompted it.** The star chart now carries the order as well as the levels: in Qwen3 4B the copying constellation resolves at **L19–22 of 36** — duplicate_word L19, induction L22, the Nevermore L22, the mover L28 — while its Basics sit at L0–1 and its Comparing rung at L5–13. Our 9m reads all three at **L1 of 6 (17%)**, because that is where the scaffold pins them; the 300k has two layers and no middle at all. The cards that separate a chaining model from ours are exactly the ones that need depth.
- **The test:** `fu-ogden-v0-1~sop003@300k-l3-pre-s1` (version 1.0.11), queued at the head. `300k-l3` — 3 layers × 64 × 2 heads, 344,512 parameters against the 300k's 295,232 — with SOP v0.0.3's content, tokeniser and passes, the same four cards, previous_word and delimiter at L0, **induction and duplicate_word at L2 (67% depth) instead of L1**.
- **The prediction, fixed now:** the four cards still read (they are pinned, and a pinned head reads the same wherever it sits); if depth is what carries chaining, the **40-item deck's gain and the four chaining tests rise above the 300k's** (gain 0.02, chaining 0.034). If the cards hold and chaining stays ~0.03, depth is not the lever at this size — and the 21m's 0.008 with its cards at L1 would say the same at 8 layers.
- **The confound, named rather than hidden:** the 3-layer shape carries 17% more parameters. If the result is positive, the parameter-matched follow-up is a 5-layer, 48-wide 300k (~270k parameters), so depth is isolated from size. A negative result needs no such control.
- **The order is now part of the standard.** SOP v0.0.3 makes the star chart a required reading, and the chart's rows are the deck's order, its columns the relations and tests, its cells the readings-and-weights. The layer a card is pinned in is the new variable; the next SOP version will carry a pinning table rather than fixed layers.

- **The deep-thin arm, queued (9 November, 07:31, Justin: "run it").** `300k-l28` — 28 layers at **24 wide**, 268,632 parameters against the keel's 295,232 — with the copy cards at **L19 (68% depth)**: Qwen3 4B's own fraction (induction L22 of 36, 61%). Version 1.0.12, queued behind the 3-layer arm. The three-point design, all near the 300k budget:

  | arm | depth × width | parameters | copy cards at | version |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | the 300k (done) | 2 × 64 | 295,232 | L1 (50%) | 1.0.0 |
  | `300k-l3` | 3 × 64 | 344,512 | L2 (67%) | 1.0.11 |
  | `300k-l28` | 28 × 24 | 268,632 | L19 (68%) | 1.0.12 |

  **Fraction held roughly constant, absolute depth 1 → 2 → 19.** That is what makes it an experiment: if chaining needs depth as *processing* (Qwen's circuit at L22 absolute), the 28-layer thin ship is the arm that shows it; if it needs *width* (the residual stream), the thin one will fail to hold its own pinned cards, which is visible in the same reading.
- **Qwen3 4B's shape, for the record:** 36 layers, 2560 wide, 32 heads (8 KV), intermediate 9,728, vocab 151,936 — its copying circuit at L19–22 (53–61% depth) and the mover at L28 (78%). A 300k at 28 layers is 24–27 wide; at 64 wide it would be 1,576,512 parameters, a ship between the 1m and the 9m.

## The SOP version is the page's filter, and depth is now part of the standard (9 November, 07:45–07:50, Justin: "with the depth section, actually the whole of the training page, you should be able to choose the SOP version and then all the charts reflect from that … is the current depth voyage still running on that version? It shouldn't be as changing anything should result in a new version … and then depth should be recorded in the SOP data model")

- **The training page reads at one version.** A SOP selector at the head of the tab narrows the run list, the loss chart, the watch and the depth panel to voyages whose spec declares that version; `''` (all versions) shows every voyage. `/api/watch`, `/api/depth` and the ladder's runs now carry and honour the version — the one leak was the watch, which was showing the running voyage whatever version was picked; it now answers `sop=0.0.1` with the finished 0.0.1 arm while `0.0.3` (or all) answers with the depth arm at sea. Verified at the endpoints.
- **A change to the standard is a new version, and that rule now has teeth.** SOP **v0.0.4** records **depth** in the data model: a `pinning` table, per size — a Basic at layer 0, a Stage 1 or a Face at two-thirds of the model's depth (Qwen3 4B: duplicate_word L19, induction and the Nevermore L22, the mover L28 of 36), the comparator at one-third, and a size with fewer than three layers at its deepest. `sop.check` reads the table and reports a spec whose layers depart from it, exactly as it reports a card out of order: a v0.0.4 spec with induction pinned at L1 is refused with `induction is pinned at L1; the depth standard puts it at L19`, while the l28's own L19 conforms.
- **What this means for what already sailed.** v0.0.1–v0.0.3 pinned every card at layer 1; those readings are the shallow baseline and are not re-read. The depth arms of today (`300k-l3` at L2, `300k-l28` at L19) were written before the table existed and are 0.0.3 sailings whose layers happen to be the new standard's — the honest record is in `ships/plans/sop-v0.0.4.md`, not in a retro-active relabel.

## The deck-stage comparison: data-taught against pin-taught at 28 layers (9 November, 08:00, Justin: "we need to be able to compare the 300k on data taught vs taught to see which produces better chaining indicators")

- **The idea.** A card can be *told* to a head (the scaffold pins attention to a metric pattern and supervises it on whatever batch is read) or *read into existence* by text whose only way through is the card. The first is what every voyage so far has done; the second has never been tried. The comparison isolates the two.
- **The corpus:** `lists/card-stage-deck-v2.csv` — 360,000 passages, 60,000 per card, in Ogden's own 850 words, each family built so one card carries the passage: plain sentences (previous_word), a repeated word (duplicate_word), a repeated phrase across two clauses (induction), two comparisons and an extreme asked for (comparator), a premise and a question about it (mover), two premises with a middle term (bridge). Written by `backend/scripts/make_card_stage.py`, deterministic, 26.7 MB, about 3% of a voyage's tokens once it reads two passes.
- **Two broken templates were caught by sampling before any run:** the first version asked its question about a *different* object than its premise, and its comparisons had no dimension to compare on. Both are fixed (mover and bridge now carry the same object through the question; the comparator asks for the extreme of the dimension it compares).
- **The arms** (both `300k-l28`, both reading the deck stage then Ogden, both v0.0.4 with the depth standard's pins in place):
  - `fu-ogden-v0-1~sop004@300k-l28-data-pre-s1` (version **3.0.0**) — the scaffold at **weight 0.0**: no pull, the cards must come from the text.
  - `fu-ogden-v0-1~sop004@300k-l28-pin-pre-s1` (version **3.0.1**) — the scaffold at **weight 1.0**: the cards pinned at L0/L0/L19/L19.
- **The reading that decides it:** the star chart's sixteen cards and the four relations, plus the four chaining tests and bpb, at the final checkpoint with no pull. If the data-taught arm reads the cards without being told, the deck can be earned from text; if only the pinned arm does, the pull is doing the work and the data stage is a curriculum rather than a teacher.
- **The weights are equal today** (60,000 passages each) because the template spaces now allow it; the per-card weighting is the next lever, and it belongs in the SOP as a `data_stage` block with explicit shares.

## The harbour page has two sections: Ships and Ship builder (9 November, Justin: "under the harbour page can we have two sections the ships and then the ship builder and the ship builder section controls all the variables in the SOP and I can define them there"; "the analogy is not harbourmaster, it's ship builder")

- **The role is ship builder, not harbourmaster.** The column is `s1.harbours.ship_builder` (migration `c8f2a4d6e1b3` renames the old `harbourmaster`), the API is `/api/shipbuilder`, and the page section is **Ship builder**. The person is the one who builds and signs the ship's supplies, not a harbour official.
- **A harbour page has two sections.** The first is **Ships** (the harbour's rules, its sizes and the cards of the ships that lie there, as before). The second is **Ship builder**: the harbour's depth and lane, the ship builder's name, and the SOP.
- **The Ship builder section controls every SOP variable.** It loads every SOP version from `s5.sop_versions` into a version selector and a JSON editor showing `content`, `parameters`, `sizes`, `cards`, `phases`, `readings`, `pinning` and `chart`. Editing and pressing **Save as a new SOP version** POSTs to `/api/shipbuilder/save`; `sop.save` validates the required variables, writes the next patch version (`0.0.4` -> `0.0.5`), and never overwrites an existing version.
- **Defining is not adopting.** The current version (`sop.CURRENT`, today 0.0.4) stays in force. A saved version appears in the selector and the standards map, but a voyage is held to it only when its spec names it (`sop: '0.0.5'`). The page says so.
- **The API:** `GET /api/shipbuilder?harbour=phaleron` returns the harbour and every version and standard; `POST /api/shipbuilder/save {harbour, standard, adopted, doc?, version?}` returns the new version and its standard. The UI calls these; the page is rebuilt in `backend/thorp/dash/static`.

## Foreign ships in Zayton (9 November, Justin: "other 'countries' ships can be seen in zayton. Like for example Qwen3 4B so I can 'spy' on it from it's page")

- **The Market Harbour lists the outside models.** `thorp/foreign.py` builds the registry from `fleet.COMPETITORS`, the reference ladder and every model with an `s4.outside_readings` reading or chain grade; Qwen3 4B, Phi-4-mini, Llama 3.2 3B, Gemma, SmolLM, Pythia and Qwen3 0.6B/1.7B appear. They are **foreign ships**: no spec, no ledger run, no weights on this Mac. `api_ladder()` carries them as `foreign`, and the Zayton page renders them below the harbour's own ships.
- **Each has a spy page.** `#/foreign/<key>` (`frontend/src/components/Foreign.astro`, `loadForeign`) reads `/api/foreign?key=...` and shows the model, parameters, held-out English bpb, the logic deck's 8- and 40-item gain/lift/score, the cards it plays and falls, the four chaining rungs, and the knockout summary. Read-only; the page says it is a spyglass, not a Thorp ship.
- **The first example** is Qwen3 4B (`#/foreign/qwen3-4b`): logic gain 0.192, lift 0.406, score 0.60, chaining lift 0.538 (the four rungs), held-out English bpb 1.074 on the 16 books. It sits in Zayton because it is far too deep for Phaleron.

- **The foreign ships have their own art.** Three were made with fal (`fal-ai/flux/dev`, the same noir style as the ships and cards): **Qwen3 4B** (`qwen3-4b`), **Phi-2** (`phi-2`) and **Phi-4-mini** (`phi-4-mini`), in `frontend/public/art/` and copied into the dashboard's static art. The other foreign ships fall back to no image (`artImg` removes a missing image), so the Market Harbour never shows a broken card. New foreign art is added to `FOREIGN` in `backend/thorp/dash/art/generate_art.py` and generated with `python backend/thorp/dash/art/generate_art.py <key>`.

## A harbour's ships and its ship builder are addresses; the SOP is tabbed (9 November, Justin: "the ships should have their own page in their own url and same thing for the shipbuilder also the sop variables need to be categorized in tabs under the shipbuilder page. Things for library, depth and 'physical' attributes")

- **The two harbour pages are routes, not hidden tabs.** `#/harbours/<harbour>/ships` is the harbour's ships; `#/harbours/phaleron/shipbuilder` is its ship builder. `#/harbours/<harbour>` canonicalises to the ships address. Zayton has no `/shipbuilder`: it has no ship builder, and the route falls back to its ships. Each individual ship already had its own address (`#/ships/<key>`), and the ship cards still open it.
- **The tabs are links.** The harbour nav sets the `ships`/`shipbuilder` links to those addresses, marks the active one, and hides the builder link where there is no builder. Nothing is hidden behind a tab state that the address cannot name.
- **The ship builder's SOP is in five tabs, and every one is available on both legs:**
  - **Library** — the leg's shelf at a glance (language, categories, size, dates, Ogdenish) and `content`/`readings`.
  - **Star chart** — `cards`, `pinning` (which card sits in which layer for each size), and `chart` (the ten-point star chart); depth is how the stars are followed.
  - **Stages** — the selected leg's stage(s): name, purpose, library, passes, tokens and cards.
  - **Phases** — the SOP's content/scaffold/content phases, their shares, and what each includes.
  - **Physical** — `parameters` (context, positions, batch, optimiser), `sizes` (each size's learning rate and warmup), and `phases` (shares, weights, holds, fades, floors). **Physical is shared by both legs; the other four follow the selected leg.**
  The version's `about` prose is shown read-only beside the version selector; `adopted`/`doc`/`supersedes` remain metadata. Every editable SOP variable is in exactly one tab. Saving still writes a **new** SOP version and does not put it in force.

## The Library tab at a glance, and the foreign flags (9 November, Justin: "in the library for the shipbuilder tab i want to immediately understand the language, and then the type of book categories in the library and it's size"; "also the date range"; "then also the ogdenish of the library"; "each ship not run by Thorp should have a flag. So google and microsoft are Silicon Valley flags. Alibaba is a chinese flag and maincoder is an aussie flag")

- **Library summary.** `library.summary(rel)` reads the shelf the SOP's `content.recipe` names and returns, from the manifest and `library_facts`: the language(s) with shares, the kinds of book (novel, stories, poem, textbook, …), the size (books, resolved, bytes, estimated tokens) and the **date range**. The `/api/library_summary?path=...` endpoint serves it; the ship builder's **Library** tab shows it above the JSON.
- **Ogdenish.** The same summary carries the Ogden-ness of the shelf: mean score, how many books are scored, and the share at the selection's 0.68 bar (`thorp/selection.py`). The Library tab shows e.g. *Ogdenish mean 0.72 · 100% at 0.68* for Ogden v0.1.
- **Flags.** Every foreign model carries a flag and a house. **Alibaba/Qwen = China**, **Microsoft/Phi and Google/Gemma = Silicon Valley** (the custom `sv.svg`), **Maincode/Maincoder = Australia**, **Meta/Llama = United States**, **Hugging Face/SmolLM = France**, **EleutherAI/Pythia and other independents = the independent flag**, and Thorp's own pathfinders carry the Thorp pennant. Flags are `frontend/public/flags/*.svg`, built into the dashboard, and appear on every foreign ship card and spy page.
- **House looks in the fal art.** `generate_art.py`'s `FOREIGN` prompts share a look per house: Qwen red battened sails, jade trim and red lanterns; Phi pale four-paned sail, slate hull and silver pennant; Gemma four-coloured sail panels and a brass compass; independent ships patched cream canvas, tarred hull and a white star. The generated art lives in `frontend/public/art/`.

- **The Library tab's book categories are the four, not the nineteen kinds** (9 November, Justin: "the kinds of books category should actually be: 1. logic and maths 2. metaphysics 3. empirical knowledge 4. general knowledge"). `library.category_of` assigns every book once: a title that is plainly logical or mathematical wins (**logic and maths**); otherwise philosophy, religion, sermons and dialogues are **metaphysics**; science, history, biography, travel, practical books and periodicals are **empirical knowledge**; the rest — novels, stories, poems, plays, essays, textbooks, reference and derived texts — is **general knowledge**. `summary()["categories"]` always returns all four in that order, with books and share; the Library tab shows them as pills. The detailed nineteen `kind`s remain in `summary()["kinds"]` for other readers.

- **The ship builder shows the two legs as tabs** (9 November, Justin: "i'd like to see the pretraining and posttraining phases as tabs"). The page has **Pretraining (outbound)** and **Posttraining (return)**, read from `s3.journeys` (`/api/shipbuilder` returns the journey as well as the SOP). The five section tabs below — Library, Star chart, Stages, Phases and Physical — are all available on both legs. Library, Star chart, Stages and Phases follow the selected leg; **Physical is shared** (one model's parameters and sizes, not a per-leg copy).

- **Depth shows the depth the SOP includes, and the ten-point chart** (9 November, Justin: "under depth, i want to understand immediately how many layers the SOP requires and then also the star chart we're looking to implement"; corrected: "the latest SOP had 28 depth and that's just included in the SOP not a separate requirement"). `starchart.depths(standard)` reads the standard's model layers per size: **2 at 300k**, 3 at 300k-l3, **28 at 300k-l28** (the latest variant's depth, deepest pinned card at L19), 4 at 1m, 6 at 9m, 8 at 21m and 12 at 50m. The **Star chart** tab headlines **28 layers at 300k-l28**, says plainly that this is the standard's included depth rather than a separate requirement, and lists every size. Below it, `starchart.POINTS` holds the ten-point chart — point, Qwen 4B evidence, deck card, and how the 300k tests it — served by `/api/shipbuilder` as `chart.points` and rendered as a table. The inspectable `pinning`/`chart` JSON stays below it. **Depth is how the stars are followed**, so the tab is the chart rather than a separate depth setting.

- **All five section tabs are for both legs** (9 November, Justin: "the library, starchart, stages, phases and pyshical tabs are for both pretraining and postraining. So we can change any one of those things i believe except for the physicalattributes actually"). **Library**, **Star chart**, **Stages** and **Phases** re-read for the selected leg — the pretraining library is `ogden`; the posttraining library is the dialogue shelf in v0.0.6 — while **Physical** is the shared model/training standard and says so. The Phases tab shows the SOP's content 40% / scaffold 40% / content 20%, with the scaffold's weight, hold, fade, floor and cards.

- **Posttraining is empty before v0.0.6** (9 November, Justin: "all the tabs for posttraining should be empty except for depth and the other things that are fixed for both post training and pretraining. We don't do any postrainign in the SOP as of today, right?"). Right for the older standards: v0.0.4/v0.0.5 have **no posttraining phases, library or stages**. From **v0.0.6** the return leg exists: the stored row carries a `post` section, and the posttraining tabs now read either `post` or `posttraining`. Older versions still leave Library, Stages and Phases empty; the shared Star chart and Physical tabs remain. The refresh/leg state fix stands. The leg selection and the open section are module state (`shipbuilderPhase`, `shipbuilderActive`), so the dashboard's periodic refresh no longer snaps Posttraining back to Pretraining.
- **SOP v0.0.6's stored `post` is the card-stage deck, not the pretraining corpus**: `post.stage = {name: 'the deck', recipe: 'lists/card-stage-deck-v2.csv', passes: 1}`, with `what`/`why` prose. The pretraining content remains `lists/ogden-v0.1.csv`; the two legs read different shelves. The earlier turn-taking draft (replay/turn-taking/calibration, `lists/dialogue-shelf.csv`) is **not** what the v0.0.6 row in the store now says; the UI was looking only for `posttraining`, which is why the stored `post` rendered blank. It now accepts both keys, and the Phases tab derives a single 100% post-training phase from the stored stage until a `phases` array is added. `sop.CURRENT` is 0.0.6.

- **The Phases tab carries the plan, not just the prose** (9 November, Justin: "the phases tab needs to show whats happening and the correct order and the tokens planend. and scaffold phases need to show which cards are being scaffolded"). It shows the phases in order as a course — 1 content 40% → 2 scaffold 40% → 3 content 20% — with each phase's `about` and its **planned tokens**. The total is the pretraining stage's tokens, or its library's estimated tokens × passes (Ogden v0.1: 51.2M × 4 passes = 204.8M, so 81.9M / 81.9M / 41.0M across the three phases). The scaffold phase lists the **cards being scaffolded** (the phase's own `cards` if it has them, else the SOP's `cards`); content phases say no cards are pulled. The tab re-reads when the selected leg or SOP version changes.

## The short voyage name: `<size>@v<SOP>`, e.g. `1m@v0.0.5` (9 November, 09:16, Justin: "the journey fu-ogden-v0-1~sop005@1m-chart-pre-s1 should just be called 1m@v0.0.5 to simplify things")

- A voyage built to an SOP version is named for the **size and the version**: `1m@v0.0.5`. A second arm of the same version carries its variation: `1m@v0.0.5-chart`, `300k@v0.0.5`, and so on. `versioning.name_of(spec)` writes the name, so a new SOP spec cannot drift back to the long form.
- The long `fu-ogden-v0-1~sop005@1m-chart-pre-s1` is the only one that survives, because it is **already running** — a voyage in flight keeps the name it was logged under. The queued 1m arm is `1m@v0.0.5` and the 300k chart arm, once it docks, becomes the reference for the short form.
- The fitout, the content and the version are all still in the spec; only the name got short. The ledger, the queue and the page all key on the name, so the short form is what will show in the run list.

## The fitout is retired; the SOP version is the voyage's identity (9 November, 09:18, Justin: "yeah fitout is retired in favour of SOP's")

- **What changed.** A voyage used to name a `fitout` (the corpus and its variations) *and* an `sop`. From v0.0.5 the SOP names the content, the tokeniser, the parameters, the phases **and** the depth, so the fitout is a legacy label: it stays readable where it is written, and is no longer required.
- **The gate now checks the SOP instead.** Three policies keyed on the fitout were taught to read it:
  - `fitout` — a voyage that names a known SOP version passes, because the SOP names the content;
  - `ogden` — passes when the SOP's content recipe is Ogden's list, not only when the fitout's key is Ogden;
  - `prechecks` — nothing keyed on a fitout, so it steps aside; the SOP's own `check` judges the voyage.
  Verified: a spec with `fitout` removed and `sop: '0.0.5'` sails through the gate clean.
- **The name follows.** `versioning.name_of(spec)` writes `<size>@v<SOP>` (with the arm as a suffix), so the identity is in the name: `1m@v0.0.5`. The `fitout` block, where it survives, is inert.
- **What this costs.** A voyage no longer records which fitout it was a variation of; the register's 1.2.3 version and the SOP version carry that instead. Nothing in the ledger is lost — the ledger never stored the fitout separately.

## Every voyage names a ship size and an SOP version (9 November, 09:32, Justin: "does it hold that any voyage needs to have a SOP version? and a ship size?")

- **It holds, and it is now enforced** as S5 policy **`standard`**: a voyage must name the ship's **size** (the model preset — the trainer cannot sail without a shape) and an **SOP version** that exists. The size gives the shape; the standard gives the content, tokeniser, parameters, phases and depth. A voyage with neither is an experiment, not a ship's voyage.
- **The cutover Justin set is honoured.** The 78 lines queued before the rule are recorded by name in `ships/specs/pre-sop.txt` and sail as they were queued ("all voyages prior to the next one queued sailed without SOP"). **Nothing new may be added to that file** — a spec with no SOP that is not on it is refused at the gate with `names no SOP version: every voyage is built to a standard`.
- **Verified both ways:** an SOP-named voyage (`300k@v0.0.5`) passes clean; a grandfathered one (`maths2@1m`) passes clean; a copy of it renamed as a new experiment is refused.
- **What it costs:** the pre-SOP tail (the maths shares, the census arms, the older pilots) can still sail, but their results are not comparable to a v0.0.5 voyage — the register marks them by their absence of a version, which is exactly what the rule is for.

## The goal is chaining, not the trigram table (9 November, 10:58, Justin: "that trigram goal isn't actually the primary goal for Pan. The primary goal for the 300k is to try get chaining at the smallest size possible. the goal of our current fleet is to get chaining equal to Qwen3 4B. We may need to analyse if chaining is the thing that most contributes to solving for quantum physics, but for now we're assuming chaining has some causation to quantum physics reasoning")

- **Pan's goal is chaining at the smallest size**, not `keyed_top1`. The bigram/trigram table stays as a reading — a 300k that holds the keys and never chains has not done its job. Augustus's maths book remains its own question; neither is the fleet's goal.
- **The fleet's goal is chaining equal to Qwen3 4B** — 0.538 on the chaining lift. Every rung is measured against that line, not against the rung below it.
- **The quantum-physics link is an assumption, recorded as one.** We assume chaining contributes to reasoning about quantum mechanics; it is not tested. A size that chains without reasoning, or reasons without chaining, is what would test it — and the fleet should be ready to find that chaining is a poor proxy.
- **What changes:** the size pages and the queue lead with chaining against Qwen3 4B (`/api/star_points` already carries the comparison), not with `keyed_top1`. The trigram table and the maths chapters stay as readings in their own sections.

## The leaderboard is three size classes, and medium and large carry five candidates to approach (11 November, Justin: "this leaderboard needs to be three tabs small models anything up to 4B medium models anything up to 30B and then large models anything above 30B ... add the 5 likelist candidates there for us to approach, same thing for the large models")

- **The classes are boundaries, inclusive at the top:** **small** up to 4B, **medium** above 4B up to 30B, **large** above 30B (`thorp/candidates.py`, `size_class`). The 4B is small (Qwen3 4B, the board leader) and a 30B is medium; a 31B is large, which is why Gemma 4 31B is the boundary case in the large list.
- **The tabs are linkable:** `/leaderboard?size=medium` and `?size=large` open the class, and clicking a tab rewrites the address. The small class keeps the measured board; a class with no chaining reading states that plainly and shows its candidates instead.
- **The five medium candidates, most likely first:** Qwen3.8 27B (the leader's own family, already on this Mac; its logic deck reads +0.133 gain and +0.449 one-hop against Qwen3 4B's +0.192 and +0.304), Qwen3 30B-A3B (the family's 3B-active MoE), Gemma 4 26B A4B (Gemma 3 4B chains at +0.254), Phi-4 14B (Phi-4-mini is second at +0.348), and gpt-oss 20B (a reasoning lineage and a post-training control).
- **The five large candidates, most likely first:** Qwen3.8 2.4T-A95B (the family flagship), DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 (the field's top open model), Kimi K3 (its strongest knowledge reading), GLM-5.3 (the top open terminal/coding result), and Gemma 4 31B (the boundary case and the only one that fits a workstation at 4-bit).
- **A candidate is a target, not a result.** It carries no chaining lift until it is scored on the four chaining tests, and joins the chaining board only then. The logic deck's gain and one-hop lift, where a reading exists, are shown beside it and labelled as the logic deck's. The order is a working order, not a result.

## ARC-AGI-2 is the canonical intelligence ranking; the Kipling ARC arm is the first attempt (11 November, Justin: "let's run the leaderboard models by ARC AGI 2 and then as part of kiplings goals get our models running so they can compete in ARC AGI 2")

- **The test:** ARC-AGI-2, the ARC Prize benchmark for fluid intelligence. Each task gives a few input/output grids and asks for the output of a new input, with no language or domain knowledge to memorise; the score is pass@2 on the 120-task evaluation set. It is the external ranking the chaining skill is ultimately measured against, not a replacement for the chaining board: ARC percentages and chaining lift are different scales.
- **The harness** (`thorp/arc.py`): the compact text form (`rows x cols:row/row/...`), the parser, exact match and cell accuracy; the data is the Apache-2.0 ARC-AGI-2 checkout, cloned read-only into `data/arc-agi-2`. The evaluator `arc` (`thorp/evaluators/arc.py`) reads a Nothing up my sleeve checkpoint with a 12-task pilot by default; `scripts/score_arc.py` reads an outside model, and `scripts/make_arc_corpus.py` writes the 1,000 training tasks as a text book and list.
- **The first readings (5 evaluation tasks, 256-token cap, pass@2):** a 1m scored 0 exact with 0 format: it has not learned the ARC form or its rules, the expected floor. Every measured 2–4B board model also scored 0 exact: Qwen3 4B, Phi-4-mini, Llama 3.2 3B, Gemma 3 4B, SmolLM3 3B, Gemma 2 2B and Phi-2. Cell accuracy separates them a little (Qwen3 1.7B 0.35, Phi-2 0.29, Gemma 3 4B and Llama 3.2 3B and SmolLM3 and Gemma 2 0.28, Pythia 410m 0.17; Qwen3 4B, Phi-4-mini, Qwen3 0.6B and Pythia 70m 0), but none writes the right grid. The published ARC-AGI-2 scores are Qwen3.8 27B 42.4, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash 1017 78.1 and Kimi K3 60.4 (BenchGecko, 2026-10-17): the canonical ranking is a reasoning-model ordering, not a small-model one. The published scores are shown on the leaderboard's Reasoning panel, apart from our own pilots.
- **Kipling's fifth aim** (added to `fleet.EXPEDITIONS["kipling"]`): build the ARC harness and corpus, train `1m@v0.0.6-arc` (Ogden, then the ARC training tasks as a post-training stage), and read the 120-task evaluation set with `arc/pass@2` as the outcome. The corpus is 922 of 1,000 training tasks (78 too long for the text budget) in one synthetic book, `arc-agi-2-train`; the arm is queued after the 21m and is the first training attempt, not a claim of parity.
- **What the harness does not do:** it does not fetch the semi-private or private sets, does not call a published score ours, and does not mix ARC percentages into the chaining table. A pilot carries its task count and is never compared with a published 120-task score.

## Arc de Triomphe: audit Qwen3.8 27B on ARC-AGI-2, then find the smallest ship above 5 (11 November, Justin: "reverse engineer Qwen's 27B architecture against the ARC-AGI-2 score ... a deep audit of every head, card, whatever that then indicates how it's working when asked the question and to determine definitely why Qwen 27 is the only model of its size that scores so high"; "taking the findings from auditing Qwen 27B and applying them against 300k, 1m, 9, and then the 21m ... we need to understand what the theoretical lowest size model is that can score above 5")

- **Registered, not launched:** `fleet.EXPEDITIONS["arc_de_triomphe"]`, status planned, after Kipling; a voyage belongs to it when its ship label starts `Arc de Triomphe`. The plan and its marks are `ships/plans/arc-de-triomphe.md`. Nothing is queued, no standard is cut and no money is spent by this entry. It is one expedition with two legs (the audit, then the ladder); the name is spelt the French way.
- **The mark:** pass@2 above 5% on the 120-task public evaluation set, which is 7 tasks or more, on two seeds. Read on the public set and labelled so; never set beside a semi-private score.
- **What was checked first (ARC Prize, verified; the model's own files on this Mac):** Qwen3.8 27B reads 42.4 at reasoning xhigh, 22.8 at low, 13.2 at medium and **1.5 with reasoning off**. The effort setting is a sentence in the system prompt, not a budget. At list price the xhigh reading is up to about 175,000 output pieces a task (derived from $0.4469 a task). The model is 64 layers, of which 48 are Gated DeltaNet (linear attention, no attention map) and 16 are full attention with 24 heads each.
- **What that changes about the brief:** the score is made in a long written trace, so the audit is of the trace and of the components at chosen positions in it, not of one forward pass; the cards and the head census read only the 384 attention heads, and the 48 DeltaNet layers need an instrument that removes and patches state, which is not built; and "the only model of its size" is tested (the same harness on Qwen3.6 27B, reported to share the configuration, and on the leaderboard's medium candidates), not assumed.
- **Four causes, stated before any reading:** the trace, the training rather than the architecture, specific components, and recall of public tasks. Each is marked held or refuted with its own reading; the plan gives the test for each. The verdict is the standard the learning ships sail under, and Justin signs it before the 300k sails.
- **The Kipling scorer cannot read this model:** `scripts/score_arc.py` caps the output at 512 pieces (256 in the pilot), shows at most three example pairs and asks for the grid only, which is reasoning off. Every 2–4B board model's 0 on the 5-task pilot was read that way, so it says nothing about what those models do when allowed to reason. The first investigation is a harness gated on task-by-task agreement with ARC Prize's published public-evaluation grid.
- **Our ships cannot see the test yet:** in the compact form at one piece a cell, 2 of the 120 evaluation tasks fit a 1,024-piece window whole, 24 fit 2,048, 87 fit 4,096 and 118 fit 8,192. The 300k that read ARC under Kipling skipped every task for length. Leg 2 fixes the window at 4,096 or more before anything else.
- **The outside floor:** a 7M recursive model is reported at about 8 and a no-pretraining model at about 4 (ARC Prize 2025 paper awards; splits not stated). Neither is a language model. The size is possible; a text model of that size doing it is what is unshown. They are read on our harness as markers.
- **The rungs:** 300k the form (format rate 0.9, cell accuracy above copying the input); 1m exact on held-out training tasks above zero on two seeds; 9m pass@2 of 2 or more of 120; 21m the mark. Each sails only if the one below clears.
- **Not decided here:** whether the audit is its own expedition; the standard's version; the spend (about $54 a full xhigh reading at list price, or a few hours of one hired 80 GB card); and whether Kipling's queued `1m@v0.0.6-arc`, with its 1,024 window, sails as it stands.

## Arc de Triomphe, revised: investigations in the queue, an instrument for the 48, Qwen3 4B first, and reasoning for our ships (11 November, afternoon, Justin: "First thing we need to do is heavily audit Qwen 27B ... We need to add audits/investigations into the queue so we can queue them alongside our ship voyages"; "we need a new instrument for the 48 before we do the audit"; "Let's start with Qwen3 4B on the Arc tests then. We'll need to test with both reasoning and no reasoning and another goal for Arc de Triomphe is for Nothing up my sleeve ships to be able to have reasoning"; on the architecture audit: "we need to do 1 - 7 and then 9, 10 and 11")

- **An investigation is a spec, queued like a voyage** (`thorp/investigate.py`, `ships/specs/investigations/`). It names its expedition outright (no label matching), its subject, the command that does the reading, a deadline and where the reading lands. `thorp queue add investigations/<name>` checks the spec and queues it; `python -m thorp.investigate list` says where each one stands. In the queue it is a `cmd:` line that runs the module, chosen so the coordinator already at sea sails it with no restart: it takes the Mac lane's seat in order with the voyages. Its deadline is kept by the runner, its log is `data/runs/investigations/<name>.out`, and its standing is in the store as `investigation-<name>`. S2 counts a queued investigation as its expedition being under way. Not done: no table of its own (the standing sits with the outside readings), and the expedition's Plan tab does not list investigations.
- **The instrument for the 48 linear-attention layers is built first** (`thorp/deltanet.py`). A Gated DeltaNet head's output is an exact weighted sum of the values written before it; those weights are its effective attention, so the cards can read a DeltaNet head. They are signed and a row does not sum to one; the instrument also gives each row as shares. Removal, mean-removal and patching are by real layer index and inside the layer, so they hold through cached generation, which the attention knockout in `thorp/inside.py` does not (that fault stands; it is item 9 of the audit). Checked against mlx-lm's own reference recurrence exactly, and on Qwen3.5 0.8B, where it rebuilds each of the 18 linear layers' own output to 0.3% of its largest value. Not yet run on the 27B (three value heads to a key head, 4-bit weights): that check is queued.
- **Qwen3 4B first, reasoning off and on.** `investigations/arc-qwen3-4b` reads the 120 public evaluation tasks twice in one load, with every example pair shown, 4,096 pieces to think and 1,024 for the grid. The thinking switch and budget in `scripts/score_arc.py` were written the same afternoon by the leaderboard session, which is also running five-task thinking pilots across the board models. The morning's zero for Qwen3 4B was its thinking, on by default, cut at 256 pieces before any grid was written: its stored outputs all begin `<think>` and none closes it.
- **The launching order changes:** Qwen3 4B on ARC, then the instrument, then the harness for the 27B's effort ladder, the ladder itself, the peers, the traces, recall, the census, the outside floor, the verdict; ten investigations, then the four ships. The census reads the 4B first, with the tools that exist.
- **A third aim: reasoning for our ships.** A Nothing up my sleeve ship that thinks before it answers: a span between two control tokens, then the answer, read the way Qwen is read, the same ship with its thinking off against on. The outcome is `arc/reasoning_gain`. Rungs: the 300k opens and closes a thinking span; the 1m shows the first gain above zero on held-out training tasks; the 9m and the 21m are read both ways. Three things must exist first: the tokens as single pieces (the Kipling session fixed, the same day, declared control tokens never reaching the tokeniser), a window with room to think, and something to learn thinking from.
- **Not decided: what a ship learns thinking from.** Worked rules written out by the generator that made the task (no modern prose; recommended first), or Qwen's own traces (what the model actually does, but another model's modern English, so an opt-in).
- **Queued, at the end:** `arc-qwen3-4b`, then `deltanet-check-qwen3.8-27b`, behind Kipling's nine lines. Moving them up is `thorp queue move`.
- **The architecture programme** is items 1 to 7, then 9, 10 and 11 of `ships/reports/architecture-and-kipling-2026-11-11.md`; item 8 (the dashboard's open port) and item 12 (backup and tests) are left out. Items 3, 4 and 6 change the run id, the metrics write and which store answers, so they wait for an empty sea. Nothing of the programme is started by this entry except the first piece of item 9 (the instrument above) and of item 11 (an investigation's spec carries `expedition:`).

## Closing Kipling: four aims, the approved queue, three holds (11 November, afternoon, Justin: "ARC-AGI-2 should be in the arc de triumphe expedition, not Kipling"; "cool queue is approved")

- **What was found first.** `300k@v0.0.6-control` topped every 300k on chaining lift (+0.160; its second seed +0.057). It is not chaining and it is not a control-token arm. (1) The lift is a scoring artefact: 51% of its 96 pairs right, median lift +0.004, logic gain −0.003 and −0.088; its per-pair spread is 3 to 4.5 times a plain 300k's, all of it in the frame-only baseline (the frame scored straight after the end-of-text mark); re-scored after a prose lead-in the lift is +0.012 and −0.010. (2) Its training tokens hold no markers: 187 `<` pieces in 74M tokens where the stamped recipe has 5,737, a tokeniser saved with `symbols: []`, and the text read `raw`. **The cause:** `train.prepare` built the tokeniser's settings from kind, vocab and byte fallback alone; `tokeniser.symbols` was read by the SOP check and never passed on. Every voyage that declared control tokens before today (the two control seeds, `1m@v0.0.6-fulldeck`, `1m@v0.0.6-early`) split them into byte pieces.
- **Fixed.** `prepare` passes the declared symbols on (`tokeniser.user_symbols`: the voyage's list without the tokeniser's own `<unk>`, `<s>`, `</s>`). A voyage that already has a checkpoint keeps the tokeniser that checkpoint was written with (`prepare(sailed=…)`), so a resume or a rescore of an earlier sailing reads it as trained. Checked on the real marks arm: 592 `<bos>`, 592 `<eos>`, 1,494 `<question>`, 1,378 `<answer>`, 1,494 `<end>`, each one piece (tokeniser `295ef56f59c9`).
- **Kipling has four aims** (`fleet.EXPEDITIONS["kipling"]`, status running): beat IFEval (rigged, and said so); prove or disprove the control tokens; a 9m that can be chatted to; the probe phase. ARC-AGI-2 is Arc de Triomphe's: `1m@v0.0.6-arc` left the queue and its ship label now begins `Arc de Triomphe`.
- **The best-in-class ship** is ten pins, each released or held as the probe reads, shared key/value heads, and the marks if they are proved. Of the five findings it was drawn from: the control-token one was the artefact above; pins hold but under a pull that reaches zero only at the last step; "release buys language" is one seed inside the seed spread (and that release lost the pins: number 2.6, order 1.7, premise 3.0); the comparator reads 1.000 with shared heads and equally with ten pins; the Bridge at 1m reached 0.977 and ended at 0.039. It is confirmed at 9m and, if it holds, sailed at 21m: the final Kipling ship.
- **The probe phase** (`optim.scaffold.probes: [{at, steps, to}]`, `train.probe_windows`): the pull pulsed off, every pinned head's distance from its card logged at every step (`scaffold/distance/<card>/l<layer>h<head>`, `scaffold/probe`). `thorp/probe_phase.py` reads each pulse (leak as a share of what the pull had won; recovery once it returns) and gives each head a word: by heart (leak under a tenth), needs the pull, lost, never took the card. The arm is `300k@v0.0.6-probe` (the ten-pin arm, 128 steps off at 10%, 20%, 40%, 60%, 80%), two seeds.
- **The control-token proof, fixed before it sails.** The metric is the **answer's own bits per byte on held-out questions** (`answers/bpb`, evaluator `answers`): for each of the 139 IFEval prompts never trained on, the loss of the answer text alone given the question, the marks in neither the count nor the bytes. **Proved** if the marked ship reads lower on both seeds and the gap between the arms is wider than the gap between the two plain seeds; otherwise not shown, and the 21m sails plain. The comparison is `300k@v0.0.6-marks` against `300k@v0.0.6` (not v0.0.5, whose pull is 10/60 where v0.0.6's is 25/100), seed for seed, and each return leg on its own pretraining ship. Beside it, not deciding: the share of answers a ship ends itself, the marks' cost in pretraining, and IFEval strict on the held-out prompts.
- **IFEval, rigged in the open.** The builder now proves the shortest response each prompt allows (`make_ifeval_corpus.shortest`): 501 of 541, 499 once read through the voyage's cleaning in both formats (it was 406; 31 prompts ask for another language and are left). The language check is seeded, so a response reads the same every time. `scripts/make_return_corpus.py` writes each prompt and response as one turn a book, marked (`<question>…<answer>…<end>`) and plain (`Question: … / Answer: …`), a split (360 train, 139 held out) and the whole (rigged). The evaluator asks in the taught format (`eval.options.ifeval`), ends the response at the ship's own end-of-text mark, and the 256-piece cap, a cost control the benchmark does not have, is lifted to 700. **The marks:** the 9m at 65% of the 541 (352 prompts); the final reading is the 21m's, against 76.89% (416). The ceiling is 92.2%.
- **The approved queue** (in `s2.queue_lines`): (1) `9m@v0.0.5-return-rigged`, the pilot on the finished 9m, also the first 9m to chat to; (2) `300k@v0.0.6` and `-s2`; (3) `300k@v0.0.6-marks` and `-s2`; (4) the four return legs; (5) `300k@v0.0.6-probe` and `-s2`, then the probe reading; **hold 1**, Justin signs the candidate; (6) `300k@v0.0.6-kipling`, two seeds; **hold 2**, it beats the ten-pin arm; (7) `9m@v0.0.6-kipling`; (8) its return leg; **hold 3**, before the 21m: the 9m chats, IFEval at 9m is 65% or better, the control-token verdict is in, the pins hold (every kept pin at half its 300k reading or better, every released pin at half its reading at release, bits per byte within 0.03 of the plain 9m's 1.501, one-hop no lower than the 300k candidate); (9) `21m@v0.0.6-kipling` and its return leg. Parked behind, not needed to close: `300k@v0.0.6-base`, the six 1m lines, `9m@v0.0.6`. Lines 6 to 9 are written at hold 1, from the probe reading.
- **A hold is a line of the queue** (`hold: <what is signed>`; `thorp queue release`, kept in the history as `released`). The lane waits at it as at any first line. The coordinator now at sea was started before the line existed and would misread it, so **hold 1 is the older form** (`cmd: while [ -e ships/specs/kipling-candidate.hold ] …`, released by removing the file); the `hold:` lines go in once the coordinator has been restarted with nothing at sea, which hold 1 itself provides.
- **Not done here:** the chaining scorer's frame-only baseline still starts at the end-of-text mark, so a ship trained on unusual text can top the board again; the question-and-answer share of the dialogue shelf is thin (3 of 78 books in catechism form), so the proof rests on the instruction turns.

## AI2 ARC: the one peer-recognised test under 1B, and the extra small board's ranking (11 November, Justin: "I'd prefer the ARC easy challenge"; "can you have the leaderboard for ARC easy and challenge be the primary leaderboard here")

- **Two benchmarks share the name, and only one has splits.** ARC-AGI-2 (Chollet; `thorp/arc.py`, `data/arc-agi-2`) is the grid puzzles already on the band's page, with training and evaluation sets and no easy/challenge split. **AI2 ARC** (Clark et al. 2018, arXiv:1803.05457) is the grade-school science test whose two splits are literally named **ARC-Easy** (2,376 questions) and **ARC-Challenge** (1,172). It is AI2 ARC that this entry makes the board.
- **ARC-Easy is the anchor and ARC-Challenge is a horizon.** Chance is 25% (four options, with 11 Easy and 7 Challenge questions carrying three or five). Easy has a gradient across the whole band; Challenge sits at or under its floor for every model trained before 2025, and Qwen3-0.6B (34.4%) is the first under 1B to clear it. So the board ranks by Easy and reads Challenge beside it, rather than pretending both rank.
- **The reading is stated in every row, because it is not the papers' reading.** Zero-shot, the option's own text as the continuation of the question, the lowest mean loss a piece the bet: no letters (a 2,048-piece Ogden vocabulary cannot be asked for "A"), no examples, no generation, greedy. It is lm-evaluation-harness's `acc_norm` in shape, not in number: published ARC scores are 25-shot and letter-scored, and none of them may be quoted beside this board.
- **Data and wiring.** `allenai/ai2_arc`, pinned to `210d026faf9955653af8916fad021475a3f00453`, CC BY-SA 4.0, fetched once into `data/arc-ai2` (`THORP_ARC_AI2` overrides). The shared plumbing is `thorp/arc_ai2.py` (data, scoring, summary), the in-run evaluator `arc_ai2`, and `scripts/score_arc_ai2.py` for an outside model, which keeps its reading as `arc-ai2-<model>` in `s4.outside_readings`.
- **BLiMP loses the title it held for a day.** It was named "the one peer-recognised test for models under 1B" on the same day; ARC takes the title because it reads what a model can do, and BLiMP stays as the grammatical reading (BabyLM's core evaluation) beside it.
- **The board is the ranking now.** `/leaderboard/xsmall` leads with AI2 ARC, ranked by Easy, with each row's questions read and skipped shown; the steps to chaining, the tenth-the-size table, the readings against size and ARC-AGI-2 follow it as the second boards. A ship of ours with no ARC reading stands below the line and is ranked on nothing. The payload's `arc_ai2` block is built once per refresh (`leaderboard.payload`), rows for the nine outside models and the five ships.
- **The first readings** (test split, zero-shot, option text, mean loss): SmolLM2-360M **64.8** Easy / 37.4 Challenge; gemma-3-270m **58.8** / 29.0; Qwen3.5-0.8B **55.3** / 34.5; Qwen3-0.6B **52.5** / 34.4; pythia-410m **45.5** / 24.9; pythia-160m **38.8** / 22.6; pythia-70m **34.3** / 21.8; pythia-31m **33.4** / 21.2; pythia-14m **29.0** / 20.7.
- **What it changes about the argument:** size is a poor predictor under 1B. A 270M Gemma beats the 600M Qwen3 by 6.3 points and a 360M SmolLM2 beats the 800M Qwen3.5 by 9.5, while the pythia ladder alone would have shown a clean size curve. That is the reading the tenth-the-size claim has to be made against, not the pythia curve.
- **Two caveats kept attached to the numbers.** Contamination is not ruled out: these are grade-school questions a pretraining corpus may hold, and the models that beat bigger ones were trained on more of the web, not better science. And the 14m and 31m readings cover 2,299 and 2,355 questions, not 2,376: on those two checkpoints a few option sequences produce a non-finite loss in float16 (reproducible on one sequence alone, so the checkpoint's own arithmetic, not the tokeniser's spelling), and the question is skipped and counted, never scored wrong.
- **Not done here:** none of our five ships has an ARC reading (each needs `arc_ai2` in its fitout and a `thorp eval`); the float32 fallback for the non-finite losses; and the readings were taken beside a voyage at sea, which cost the 1m a nine-minute stall before the scorer's MLX buffer cache was capped at 1 GB and the batch cut to 32.

## Arc de Triomphe gains a fourth aim: the AI2 ARC audit (11 November, Justin: "can you add it as a goal to the arc de triomphe expedition"; "talk me through how we can audit all those results on the 9 foreign ships to see if we can figure out where our best ship is against SmolM2 360m")

- **The aim, in the expedition's own words** (`fleet.EXPEDITIONS["arc_de_triomphe"].goals`, the table under the goal): the nine outside AI2 ARC readings taken apart — a per-question receipt for every reading, a determinism re-run, the format ablations and an answer-swap contamination probe — then our own five ships read on the same test, the same format and the same normalisation, so the band has one reading with a gradient under 1B and the tenth-the-size claim has something to say. ARC-AGI-2 gives nothing at this size (every model under 4B solves 0 of the 120 tasks), so the band's ranking rests entirely on AI2 ARC and an unaudited ranking there is a claim.
- **Four goals now, not three.** The other three are unchanged and now written into the same table (the audit of Qwen3.8 27B, the arch — smallest ship above 5 — and our first reasoning model), each with its detail and its why. The goal text's "Three aims" became "Four aims", with the fourth added after them.
- **Four steps behind it, numbered 15 to 18 and out of leg 1's order.** 15: a receipt a reading (the per-question rows `arc_ai2.score_questions` already builds and `scripts/score_arc_ai2.py` threw away) — so any number can be re-derived and any two models compared question by question. 16: determinism (greedy, fixed order: a re-run must be bit-identical, so drift is a bug) and the four format ablations (mean against sum loss, options shuffled, the question stripped, the no-model baselines). 17: the normalisation our ships can share — bits per byte against mean loss a piece, because a 2,048-word Ogden vocabulary spells *evaporation* as six cheap byte pieces where SmolLM2 spells it as one expensive word piece. 18: the answer-swap probe on the two models whose order decides the board, then `arc_ai2` in the five ships' fitouts and the pairing, 50m at 41M against SmolLM2-360M at 360M (8.8×), with SmolLM2 also read on the chaining deck so each stands on two measures.
- **It is independent of the ladder and says so.** The stop rule now reads: the audit (15–18) may run at any time and blocks no ship — it reads a test and trains nothing. It is the one aim in the expedition that can be finished this week.
- **Two outcomes added** so the page can read it: `arc_easy/accuracy` (AI2 ARC-Easy zero-shot, against SmolLM2-360M's 64.8% at 8.8 times the size; the mark is set after the audit, not before) and `arc_challenge/accuracy` (the floor beside it, which no model trained before 2025 has cleared).
- **The plan document** (`ships/plans/arc-de-triomphe.md`) gains "The four aims" and a "Leg 4 — the AI2 ARC audit" section with the checks, the board as it stands, and the four things that would overturn it: SmolLM2-360M falling to the length or first-option baseline; the 9.5-point gap over Qwen3.5-0.8B inverting under sum loss or shuffled options; the ordering breaking under bits per byte; or the answer swap leaving its accuracy high, which would say the reading is recall and not science.
- **Not decided:** the mark our ship has to read on ARC-Easy to count as matching a model 8.8 times its size; whether the audit's receipts change the `arc_ai2` artifacts' shape for every future reading (they should); and whether SmolLM2-360M's chaining reading is run on the logic deck in-run or through the outside-model scorer.

## The queue by goal, and closed expeditions out of planning (11 November, Justin: "you shouldn't be able to plan an expedition that is closed, also the queue has to be more flexible. So it has to be ordered by goals within an expedition. So I could run an ARC-AGI audit as a goal under arc de triomphe and then next a knockout expedition goal"; and "let's move The knockout expedition to currently sailing")

- **A priority row is an expedition or one of its goals** (`thorp/priorities.py`): the key is `arc_de_triomphe` for an expedition that states no goals, and `arc_de_triomphe:4` for the fourth goal of one that does. An expedition with goals is ranked one row a goal, so a goal of one expedition can sit between two goals of another — the ARC audit (`arc_de_triomphe:4`) then the knockout's SmolLM2 goal (`knockout:2`), which the old expedition-level order could not express. A spec joins a goal by naming it (`goal: 4` in the fitout's YAML); a line with no goal sits with its expedition's first row, so an unmarked queue still sails in expedition order.
- **An old order keeps its meaning.** A bare expedition key saved before this expands in place to its goals, so the file written yesterday still orders the queue the way it read. Proven against a scratch order: `arc_de_triomphe:4` → `knockout:2` → everything else, and `["knockout", "arc_de_triomphe"]` expanding to `knockout:1, knockout:2, arc_de_triomphe:1, …`.
- **A closed expedition cannot be planned.** `priorities.closed()` reads the fleet's own word for it (`status == "done"`); the priority page does not offer those rows and the save drops them rather than refusing the whole order. `book_value` and `apprentice` are the two closed expeditions today, and they left the list (26 rankable rows → 24 shown). The rule is deliberately keyed to `done` and not to S2's `home`: home means nothing is at sea this minute, done means the expedition will not sail again.
- **The knockout states two goals.** (1) The question it was built for: are the four cards causally necessary. (2) **Make the 21m smoke SmolLM2-360M**: read SmolLM2-360M — 64.8% on AI2 ARC-Easy at 360M, the best under 1B and the model the band's board now ranks first — the way the knockout reads a model that chains, silencing the heads its own cards name and measuring what its ARC reading loses against random heads, then carrying what holds into the 21m. The 21m is 17 times smaller, so a size curve cannot get there and a causal census can.
- **"Currently sailing" is S2's view and nothing else** (corrected the same minute: Justin, "first light and two fact problem are not currently sailing"). A declared `running` is what the fleet meant when it was written, not what is sailing: `first_light` and `two_facts` carry it and have nothing queued, and counting it put them under the helm with the 1m. The band is `at sea` or `under way`, where under way means a specline the spec names it, or an investigation whose spec names it (vsm._compute_expedition_states).
- **The knockout is under way because it has work queued, not because of a word.** A bare `cmd:` line names no expedition to the state machine, so `thorp queue add "cmd: ... -m thorp.knockout ..."` left it "awaiting orders". The supported way is an investigation: `ships/specs/investigations/knockout-smollm2-360m.yaml` (`expedition: knockout`, subject SmolLM2-360M), queued with `thorp queue add investigations/knockout-smollm2-360m --top`, which the coordinator reads as `python -m thorp.investigate run knockout-smollm2-360m`. It is first in the queue, so it sails when the 9m return-rigged docks.
- **Not done:** nothing is queued for either goal yet — the ARC audit (`arc_de_triomphe:4`) and the knockout reading (`knockout:2`) are rows Justin can now order, but neither has a line under it, and no line names a goal yet, so today's queue still sails in expedition order until a fitout says `goal: n`.

## Chat becomes a primary surface: a permanent left rail of conversations (11 November, Justin: "Chat has to be a primary component of the UI. So it needs to have a permanent left sidebar which when opens shows all the recent conversations by model")

- **A conversation is one model**, keyed by what the model is — `foreign:gemma-3-270m`, `ship:300k:2980e0eca706` — not by the run it happens to be sailing, so a ship that re-sails keeps its history. Kept in this browser (`localStorage`, slot `thorp.chat.conversations.v1`), because a reader's history is theirs and the dashboard has no account to hang it on; nothing is sent anywhere.
- **The rail is permanent and collapsible.** Open, it lists every conversation newest first: the model, whether it is ours or foreign, its turns, when it was last used and its last line; a click opens it, the × forgets it, `+ New chat` starts over on the current model. Closed, it is a 46px strip with the count, so the chat gets the width back. On a narrow screen it stacks above the chat instead of beside it.
- **Saving happens where the messages change, not in an effect on `msgs`.** An effect would run on the render that switches target and write the previous model's messages under the new model's key; it did, in the first cut, and the fix is `persist()` called inside the two `setMsgs` in `send`. The load effect is the only reader.
- **Caps and a quota path.** 40 messages a conversation, 30 conversations; a write over quota drops the audits first (the big part, and re-readable by asking again), then everything but the ten newest. A browser that refuses to keep them still chats — the history is what is lost.
- **Opening a conversation from another page's rail navigates** to that model's chat (`chatHref`, the same rule the ⌘K palette uses), because an embedded chat is pinned to its own model.
- **Not done:** no server-side history, so conversations do not follow a reader between browsers; no renaming or pinning; the rail lists one conversation a model, so a second chat with the same model replaces the first's stored copy rather than sitting beside it.

## Saving a chat: threads, not one conversation a model (11 November, Justin: "ok so we also need an ability to save a chat then")

- **A chat is a thread with an id and a title.** The first cut keyed a conversation by the model alone, so a second chat with the same model replaced the first — fine until a chat is worth keeping. Now every chat is written as it happens (a draft, titled by its first question, first line, cut at 60 characters), and **Save** names it and marks it: a saved thread is never overwritten by the next chat with that model, never pruned, and carries a ★ in the rail. Drafts are pruned oldest-first past 20; 60 threads in all; 40 messages a thread.
- **The rail groups by model**, the model with the newest thread first, each model's threads newest first, saved and draft together. Opening one from an embedded chat navigates to that model's page (an embedded chat is pinned to its own model); on `/chat` it switches target and thread in place.
- **The v1 store migrates once**, its one-conversation-a-model entries arriving as saved threads under `thorp.chat.threads.v2`; a single row a model becomes a single row with a star, so nothing a reader had is lost by the change.
- **Writing still happens where the messages change**, through a `msgsRef` so the async answer is appended to the thread the question went into, not to whatever `msgs` the closure saw when the request went out.
- **Not done:** no rename after saving (only naming at save), no pinning, and no server-side history — the threads live in this browser.

## The chat console: a carved drawer at the bottom of every page (11 November, Justin: "There needs to be a permanent chat model that slides up from the bottom of the screen. Very elegant, fal image of wooden carvings like a beautiful ship navigation panel built in the hey day or the merchant navy days. It slides up and then it's the chat console, where you can choose any instruct model you want, see the context window, and also save notes")

- **It is chrome, not a page**: `ChatConsole` is mounted in `Layout.astro` beside the ⌘K palette, so it is on every page. Closed it is a 54px rail with a brass nameplate; ⌘J (or a click, or esc to close) slides it up to `min(62vh, 560px)` over 320ms. `body` keeps a 56px bottom pad so no page ends under the rail.
- **The plate is Eduardo Risso, made with fal** (Justin, same day: "the chat modal needs to be in the style of risso"). "Risso" is the house style already recorded in `thorp/dash/UI.md` and `art/generate_art.py`: Eduardo Risso's noir comic art — heavy black ink, stark chiaroscuro, one light source, near-black ground, no text. The console's subject is added to that generator (`CONSOLE`, key `chat-console`, `landscape_16_9`) and generated with `fal-ai/flux/dev`: a merchantman's navigation console, carved black oak, a binnacle, dividers on an open chart, a brass lantern. It is saved to `frontend/public/art/chat-console.png` (the fal reply is JPEG data even when asked for a png — converted with `sips`) and the bar paints it at `center 72% / cover` under a left-to-right scrim, so the nameplate stays legible. The drawn SVG (`Woodwork`) stays in the component, hidden by CSS, as the fallback when the file is absent.
- **The console's chrome is Risso too**: near-black panels, bone and brass type, uppercase buttons at 11px with hairline brass borders, one brass rule where the panel meets the bar. The drawer is `flex-direction: column-reverse`, so the handle is at the bottom and the panel rises above it rather than under it.
- **A lab, then a model** (Justin: "in the model selection it's a sequence. Choose the lab. and then choose the relevant model. Only show instruct models, so no Thorp model exists yet"). The picker is two selects: the lab (Alibaba, Google, Hugging Face, Meta, Microsoft, EleutherAI, OpenAI, Maincode, Other — the same families as `thorp/labs.py LABS` and the board's `MAKER`), then the models that lab makes. Only instruction-tuned outside models are offered — 12 of them today: Alibaba 6, Google 3, Microsoft 2, Hugging Face 1 — because a base checkpoint continues prose instead of answering and no Nothing up my sleeve ship is instruction-tuned yet, so a Thorp row would be a lie.
- **The same parts as the chat page, re-used rather than re-built**: the same `/api/chat`, the same conversation store (a console chat *is* a thread, saved and listed on the chat page too), and the same `ContextMeter` — the window, what this session has used, what is left after a full answer, and the last turn's split, on hover.
- **The thinking switch** (Justin: "it needs a thinking button" then "switch"): a real switch in the console's header, not a button that toggles a word. It follows the model — the eight instruct checkpoints whose template has an off switch (all the Qwens, both Gemma 4s) start on their own default; the four with no reasoning mode (Phi-4-mini, Phi-4 14B, Gemma 3 270M Instruct, SmolLM2-360M Instruct) show it off and inert with the reason on hover; a template that always reasons would show it on and inert. The request carries `thinking: 'on'|'off'` from the switch, so an answer is asked for the way the chat page asks.
- **Notes** (`thorp/notes.ts`, `thorp.chat.notes.v1`): a line kept from an answer, with the model it was written against and the thread when there is one; 300 kept, deletable. They sit in a 300px drawer beside the conversation and stack under it on a narrow screen.
- **Not done:** tools beyond notes (no pinning a note into a thread, no export), no keyboard history recall, and the console keeps its own selection rather than following the chat page's.

## The art direction is two-part: Risso's ink, Drake's world (11 November, Justin: "can you audit all the images and the instructions and memory to make sure that we use the Risso artstyle from 100 bullets but we now also have a time period and it's from the days of Drake. So all images, everything should be a homage to the heyday of Sir Francis Drake but drawn by risso's style")

- **"Risso" was already the house style, and it was undocumented in one place**: `thorp/dash/UI.md` and `art/generate_art.py` both name Eduardo Risso's noir comic art (100 Bullets), and every image since 7 November was made in it with `fal-ai/flux/dev`. What was missing was the period.
- **`STYLE` now carries both halves**, so every future generation inherits them: the Risso clause as before, then "set in the heyday of Sir Francis Drake, Elizabethan England 1577-1596: oak and tarred rope, canvas and brass, lanterns and tallow candles, period shipwrights' and navigators' tools, doublets and sea-cloaks, nothing modern, no anachronism". The module docstring records the rule and its one exception: a ship of another nation's build is allowed as a *prize* (Drake took the São Filipe in 1587), because that is the period rather than outside it.
- **The audit** (`ships/reports/art-audit-2026-11-11.md`): 103 subjects across 15 groups, every one with an image on disk. **20 are not in Drake's world as written** and are listed with a proposed Elizabethan rewrite each: the three harbours (Phaleron, Piraeus, Zayton — Greek and ancient trading ports), Plato, Diderot, Augustus and Pan; nine Qwen subjects (Chinese junks and treasure ships) and three Gemma subjects (Venetian carracks); two Phi subjects (Byzantine dromons).
- **Every image predates the period**, because they were generated before the era clause existed. The console's plate (`chat-console`) was regenerated first as the proof of the new style: a chart table with rolled charts, a brass lamp and candles under the rigging, no anachronism. The other 103 are due one regeneration pass (`python -m thorp.dash.art.generate_art`), overwriting in place, about ten minutes of fal time.
- **Not done:** the 20 conflicting prompts have not been rewritten in the generator, and none of the 103 images has been regenerated. Both are editorial decisions — the Qwen junk and the Gemma carrack are how the harbour says *which lab made the model*, so replacing them with English hulls is a change of meaning, not only of period, and is Justin's to sign.

## The art brief is three layers, and the naming has to follow (11 November, Justin: "actually the style is rissos, the inspriation is drake and the merchant ships exploring new worlds, the world is banks early days of the culture"; then "naming must change and art style accordingly")

- **Corrected from the last entry**: the period is not literally 1577–1596. The brief is three layers — **style** Risso's ink; **inspiration** Drake and the merchant ships that went looking for new worlds; **world** Iain M. Banks' Culture in its first days, a young and expanding civilisation. The age of sail is the ship; the Culture is the universe. `STYLE` now carries all three, so a generation inherits them without a per-subject reminder: the Culture appears only as its material traces — an orbital ring where a moon would be, a distant habitat, a small silver drone at rest on the chart or in the rigging.
- **The console's plate was regenerated as the proof**: a chart table aboard a ship of Drake's kind, lanterns and brass and rolled charts, and in the sky beyond the rigging a moon and a hovering structure that is not of this century. `frontend/public/art/chat-console.png`.
- **A syntax error I introduced is repaired**: the first pass at this edit left a stray block after `STYLE`'s closing parenthesis; `generate_art.py` imports clean again (103 subjects, ruff clean) and the regenerated plate is the confirmation.
- **Naming is now open, and it is not a rename of labels**: the world's names are still the merchant navy's — harbours called Phaleron, Piraeus and Zayton; expeditions named Kipling, Arc de Triomphe, The knockout; classes named keel, pilot, speller, stylist, paragraph, explorer, trader; 103 art keys tied to model ids, expedition keys and card keys. Moving them into the Culture's naming touches URLs (`/harbours`, `/expeditions/<key>`, `#/leaderboard/lab/<key>`), spec files, the store's keys and the art filenames at once, so it is a migration with a decision behind it, not a find-and-replace.
